### <span class="fac">[70\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/70.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> To say of the hollow cylinder that it is ‘unsatisfied’—what is that supposed to mean! – But I can imagine that _instead_ _of_ the expression “the empty hollow cylinder,” one might use the expression “the unsatisfied hollow cylinder,” & that one _might call_ the solid cylinder that fits into it “its satisfaction.”

### <span class="fac">[70\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/70.webp),[71\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/71.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> A wish seems to already know what will fulfill it, or would; the sentence, the thought, what makes it true, even if it is not yet there! Where does this _determination_ come from, of that which is not yet there? This despotic disposition?

And where does this strange sensory illusion come from? – We say that the sentence says something; the wish wishes, the command commands something. But how do we use these statements, when do we use them, in what broader context? _What_ is it that a sentence says; what do we put in place of “something”? This sentence says: that… – and then follows another sentence. “The sentence says something,” to which the complement is either the question “What?” and another sentence – or the expression “says something” is not a variable, does not mean: says _this_, or _that_.

### <span class="fac">[71\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/71.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> ‘The command commands its obedience.’ Yes, so it already knows its obedience before it _is_ there! – But the sentence is just a grammatical one about the words “command” and “obedience.” It says: If a command says: “Do this and that,” then “doing this and that” is called obeying this command. (Similarly, the sentence: “The dog has ‘legs,’ the hare has ‘paws.’”)

### <span class="fac">[71\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/71.webp),[72\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/72.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> We say: “The command commands _this_” and do it; but also: “the command commands this: I should do this and that.” We translate it once into a sentence, once into a demonstration, and once into a deed.

### <span class="fac">[72\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/72.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Yes, it already commands – I would like to say – that I should do that! But what is _that_? I am hypnotized by the form: “It commands _this_.”

### <span class="fac">[72\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/72.webp),[73\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/73.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> One could also say: This command commands _this_ (one does it) – – but hasn’t it already commanded this before? (It didn’t command anything else before, did it!) So it commanded this act before it (yet) _existed_. But in what sense did it command this earlier? – Is commanding an activity that the command also performed earlier? And how did it perform it? “The command commands this and that” does not contain time at all, any more than “2 + 2 is 4.” “I also meant _this_ earlier” does contain time. – How can one mean what has not yet happened? But what did it consist of back then: to mean _this_? What, then, do we now call: ‘having meant _this_, which is now happening’? What does the identity consist of: _doing the same thing_ now that I _meant_ earlier? What does it consist of: preparing the _same_ food now that I will eat later?

### <span class="fac">[73\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/73.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Yes, I already mean now what I will later carry out. – Yes, sometimes I mean the same thing; _sometimes something else_! In which cases do we say the one, and in which cases the other? In which cases do I say that I have done something _different_ from what I meant – & in which the same thing. And if the command is _not_ followed – where then is the shadow of its following, which you meant to see; because the form was in your mind: It commands _this & that_.

### <span class="fac">[73\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/73.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> _How does one do it_: to command _this & that_?

### <span class="fac">[73\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/73.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> One says: one commands _the command_, & also: one commands _the action_.

### <span class="fac">[74\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/74.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> We identify the sentence “that …” with the action.

### <span class="fac">[74\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/74.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “He did _that_ which I commanded him to do” – Why should one not say here, that it is an identity of the action & the _words_?! Why should I put a shadow between the two? We _have_ a method of projection. – Only it is a different identity: ‘I did what he did’, & on the other hand: ‘I did what he commanded’.

### <span class="fac">[74\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/74.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Rule: “the wish that p be the case = the wish that is satisfied by p being the case”.

### <span class="fac">[74\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/74.webp),[75\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/75.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">4</span> (And here I mean: the expectation is unsatisfied, because it is the expectation of something; the belief, the opinion unsatisfied, because it is the opinion that something is the case, something real, something outside the process of the opinion.)

### <span class="fac">[75\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/75.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> To understand a sentence means to understand a language. Something is a sentence only in a language.

### <span class="fac">[75\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/75.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> What does it mean when one says: “I cannot imagine the opposite of that”, or: “What would it be like if it were different?” – e.g., if someone has said that my mental images are private, or that only I myself can know whether I feel pain, & the like.

### <span class="fac">[75\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/75.webp),[76\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/76.webp),[77\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/77.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> “I cannot imagine …” here naturally does not mean: My powers of imagination are not sufficient. – We use this kind of statement to lead an assertion to absurdity, when this assertion is a grammatical one, which gives us the appearance of an assertion about something factual. But why do I say: “I cannot imagine the opposite”; why not: “I cannot imagine _that_ (what you say)”? An example: “Every rod has a length” – that is to say: we call something (or, _this_) ‘the length of a rod’ (but nothing ‘the length of a sphere’). Can I now imagine that ‘every rod has a length’? Well, I just imagine a rod – & that is all. Only this image plays a completely different role in connection with this sentence than an image in connection with the sentence: “my table has the same length as yours”. For here I understand what it means to form an image of the opposite (and it does not have to be a mental image). But the image of the grammatical sentence could only serve to show what is called ‘the length of a sentence’. And what would the opposite image of that be?

### <span class="fac">[77\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/77.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> It seems as if one could say: “Word-language allows nonsensical word combinations, but the language of mental images does not allow nonsensical images.” – So the language of drawing does not allow nonsensical drawings either? Suppose they are drawings according to which bodies are to be modeled. Then, for example, the drawing

![](../graphics/116,77-1.svg)

has sense, and the drawing

![](../graphics/116,77-3.svg)

has no sense. What if I imagine nonsensical word combinations!

### <span class="fac">[77\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/77.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> How does one show the nonsensicality of a sentence, e.g. “This body has extension”, by saying: “I cannot imagine how it could be different”? <s> We tend to see that sentence as the statement of something self-evident. We would like to say: “Yes, of course it has extension; but how could it be different; so why say it?” </s>

### <span class="fac">[78\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/78.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> We could respond to the sentence “This body has extension” with: “Nonsense!”, but we tend to respond: “Of course!”. Why?

### <span class="fac">[78\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/78.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “I have actually never seen a black spot gradually become lighter and lighter until it is white, & then the white become more and more reddish until it is red; but I know that it is possible, because I can imagine it.” How do I know that it is possible because I can imagine it? (“I know that it is possible to unlock this lock with the bolt, because I have unlocked such locks in this way before.” Do I assume, in this sense, that this color transition will be possible because I have imagined it?)

### <span class="fac">[78\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/78.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> When it is said that a sentence is nonsensical, it is not as if its sense is nonsensical. Rather, the sentence is excluded from the language.

### <span class="fac">[79\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/79.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> I try to do something, but I cannot. – But what does it mean to “not be able to try to do something”? “We cannot even _try_ to imagine a round square.”

### <span class="fac">[79\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/79.webp),[80\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/80.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> Even if one regards the sentence as a picture of a possible state of affairs & says that it shows the possibility of the state of affairs, the sentence can at best do what a painted or sculptural image, or a film, does, & it cannot, in any case, present what is not the case. So it depends entirely on our grammar what is called (logically) possible, and what is not—namely, precisely what it permits. But that is arbitrary! – In _a_ sense, _yes_. But I cannot make use of every sentence-like construction; not every game is useful, and when I am tempted to accept something completely useless as a sentence, it usually happens because I have not thought through its application sufficiently. For example, when I speak of an ‘infinitely long row of trees’ and do not ask myself how one might verify that a row of trees is infinitely long.

### [PG I App IV](/w-pg-1-app-4/#ms-116-80.2+81.1+82.1) <span class="fac">[80\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/80.webp),[81\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/81.webp),[82\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/82.webp)</span> {#802811821}

<span class="series-number">1</span> If one wants to use the designation “elementary proposition” as I have done in the Logical-Philosophical Treatise, that is, as “atomic proposition” in Russell’s sense, then one can call the proposition “here stands a red rose” an elementary proposition. That is, it contains no truth-function and is not defined by an expression that contains one. But if one is to say that a proposition is an elementary proposition only if its logical analysis does not bring a truth-function to light, this presupposes that one has a conception of such an ‘analysis’. I myself spoke in earlier times of “complete analysis,” in the thought that philosophy must definitively dissect all propositions in order to clarify all connections and eliminate every possibility of misunderstanding. As if there were a calculus in which such a dissection were possible. I had in mind something along the lines of Russell’s definition of the definite article. Similarly, I once thought, one could and should define the concept of a sphere with the help of visual images, etc., and thus the connection between the concepts—the source of all misunderstandings, etc.—would be demonstrated once and for all. Underlying this (for everyone) was a falsely idealized image of language and its use. Of course, in certain cases one can clarify the connection between the various ways expressions are used through definitions. And even in the case of the connection between ‘visual image’ and ‘sphere,’ such a definition can help. But not by defining the concept of the physical sphere, rather by describing a language-game related to ours—or rather, a whole series of language-games related to it—and such definitions may occur within these. This juxtaposition destroys grammatical prejudices and enables us to see the use of a word as it really is, rather than _attributing_ a use to the word.

### [PG I App IV](/w-pg-1-app-4/#ms-116-82.2) <span class="fac">[82\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/82.webp)</span> {#822}

<span class="series-number">1</span> There may, under certain circumstances, be a calculus that decomposes sentences, and it is not difficult to imagine such a thing. It is then a calculation to determine whether a sentence is an elementary sentence or not. The question of whether a logical product (e.g.) is hidden in a sentence is a mathematical problem. – What is meant here by “hidden” is defined by the method of searching for what is hidden (or rather, by the lack of a method).

### <span class="fac">[82\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/82.webp),[83\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/83.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “When I say, ‘I did _not_ dream last night,’ I must nevertheless know where to look for the dream (i.e., the sentence ‘I dreamed’ may, on application to the actual situation, be false but not nonsensical).” Does this mean, then, that you did feel something—so to speak, a hint of a dream—that makes the spot where a dream _would_ have been conscious? Or, if I say, “I have no pain in my arm,” does that mean I have a shadow of a sensation of pain that indicates the spot where the pain would occur? – To what extent does the present, pain-free state contain the possibility of pain? If someone says, “For the word ‘pain’ to have meaning, it is necessary to recognize pain as such when it occurs,” one can reply: “It is no more necessary than recognizing the absence of pain.”

### <span class="fac">[83\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/83.webp),[84\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/84.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> “But don’t I have to know what it would be like if I were in pain?” – One cannot escape the fact that using the sentence involves imagining something with every word. The application of the sentence is _not_ the one that requires such an imagination. Time and again, one tends to think of the sense of a sentence—that is, its use (its purpose)—as concentrated in a mental state of the speaker or listener. One does not think that one _calculates_ with the words, operates on them, or substitutes this or that image for them over time. Rather, their sense—that is, their purpose—is supposed to lie in a kind of image that they produce in the speaker’s mind. It is as if one believed that, for example, the written instruction to follow a cow—which someone is supposed to carry out for me—must always be accompanied by a mental image of a cow if this instruction does not lose its sense. By this I do not mean, of course, that in certain language-games it is not essential to make the transition from words to mental images at certain points. – When we communicate to the doctor that we are in pain—in which cases is it useful for him to imagine pain? – –And by the way, what is it like to imagine pain? Does this not happen in a great variety of ways? (As varied as remembering a pain.)

### <span class="fac">[85\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/85.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> The feeling is that, in order to deny ‘p’, one must first, in a certain sense, make it true. One asks: “_What_ is not the case?” This must be represented. – But it _is_, after all, represented by p.

### <span class="fac">[85\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/85.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> One cannot get away from the fact that the meaning of the sentence accompanies the sentence; it is with the sentence.

### <span class="fac">[85\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/85.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Is the negation of a sentence identical with the disjunction of not-excluded cases? It is in some cases. (E.g., in this: “The permutation of the elements A, B & C, which he wrote down, was not ACB.”)

### <span class="fac">[85\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/85.webp),[86\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/86.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">4</span> To negate: a ‘mental activity’. Negate something – & observe what you are doing. – Do you perhaps shake your head inwardly? And if so – is this now more worthy of our interest than, say, writing a ‘~’ in front of a sentence? Do you now know the _essence_ of negation?

### <span class="fac">[86\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/86.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> “How can the word ‘not’ negate?!” “The sign ‘not’ indicates that you should interpret what follows negatively.” One might say: “The sign of negation is merely an invitation to do something, possibly quite complicated.” It is as if the sign of negation prompts us to do something; but what? That is not said. It is as if it only needs to be hinted at; as if we already know it. As if an explanation were unnecessary, since we already know the matter.

### <span class="fac">[86\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/86.webp),[87\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/87.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> What is the difference between these two processes: to wish that something happen & to wish that the same thing _not_ happen? If one wants to represent it figuratively, one will use the image of the event to do something: to cross it out, to strike it through, & the like. But that, it seems to us, is a _crude_ method of expression. In the word-language, we even use the word “not”; this is like a clumsy expedient. One feels: in _thought_, it already happens differently.

### <span class="fac">[87\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/87.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Negation, one might say, is an excluding, rejecting, gesture. But we use it for _many_ things.

### <span class="fac">[87\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/87.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “Is it the _same_ negation when one says: ‘Iron does not melt at 100˚ C’ & ‘2 × 2 is not 5’?” Is this ascertainable through introspection, while saying the two sentences?

### <span class="fac">[87\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/87.webp),[88\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/88.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> The word sometimes seems to carry its meaning with it, as if it were something placed in this vessel. We now see it used in two sentences & ask naively: does it contain the same thing in this as in that?

### <span class="fac">[88\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/88.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Is ‘understanding a word’ a state of mind? – We call sadness and excitement states of mind. We say: “He was very sad all day,” “He has been in great excitement since yesterday,” “He has had uninterrupted pain since yesterday.” We also say: “I have understood this word since yesterday,” if it was explained to me yesterday; but did I understand it continuously? Yes, one could speak of an interruption in the understanding if I once forgot it & then learned it again; but can one ask: “At what time did you stop understanding it?” as one can ask: “At what time did your pain subside?”?

### <span class="fac">[88\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/88.webp),[89\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/89.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “I have understood the word ‘valve’ since yesterday.” – Have you felt the understanding all the time? You have been able to use the word since yesterday; just as you have been able to use thousands of other words for a long time. If one wants to call the understanding of the word a state of mind, then in the sense of certain _abilities_. E.g. the ability to calculate, to speak a language, to play chess, to recite a poem. One can say of each of these sentences that it describes a state of mind: “I have had pain since yesterday,” “I have been expecting him since yesterday,” “I knew since yesterday that he would come,” “I have been able to integrate since yesterday.”

How has this played out in each of these cases?

### <span class="fac">[89\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/89.webp),[90\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/90.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> What do we regard as a criterion for the fact that we can now play chess; what for the fact that we now know this poem by heart? – But what is my criterion for the fact that I now have pain? Pain, sorrow, anger, etc. I want to call “states of consciousness”; in contrast to the knowledge of the multiplication table, to the ability to play chess, to integrate, to use the chemical symbols & the more common words of the German language: these I want to call “hypothetical states of the mind.” One could also call them hypothetical states of a model of the mind. And the brain & nervous system can also function as a model of the mind.

### <span class="fac">[90\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/90.webp),[91\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/91.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Perhaps one might want to apply the words “conscious” & “unconscious” states of the mind here. Be my guest, but nothing is more misleading than that! I can also talk about “conscious” & “unconscious” toothache; the sentence: “I have unconscious toothache” can, for example, mean something like: “I have a bad tooth & no pain.” But “conscious state” & “unconscious state,” this inevitably associates itself with the image: a thing that we see, & a similar thing that we do not see, because it is, for example, behind us. But this comparison is quite misleading.

### <span class="fac">[91\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/91.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> ‘Being able to climb a mountain’ can be called a state of my body. I say: “I can climb up—I mean, I’m strong enough to do it.” But it is a state of a different kind when I say: “Yes, I can go there—I mean, I have time to do it.” – <s> “But if you can really do it, then **all** the conditions must be met; then ‘you can do it’ means not only that you have the time, but also that you have the strength, and so on.” </s>

### <span class="fac">[91\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/91.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “But what a strange state it is: _‘being_ _able_ _to_ do something’! How does he know that what he is doing now is: _being able to_ do the other thing that he is not doing now?”

### <span class="fac">[91\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/91.webp),[92\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/92.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Someone claims that he can do something specific; he tries it now, & it does not succeed. Imagine circumstances under which it makes sense to say: “When I said I could do it, I really could, but now I can’t” – & circumstances under which this does not make sense.

### <span class="fac">[92\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/92.webp),[93\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/93.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> In response to the question: “Can you lift this weight?”, the answer might be: “I don’t know; we’ll see.” The same applies to the question: “Can you still play chess?” But if someone asks me, “Do you have understanding of the word ‘tree’?”—can I say, “We’ll see”? – The answer would be: “You must _know_ whether you have the understanding or not!” But suppose the question had been: “Do you know how a pawn moves in chess?” and he had answered: “We’ll see”—then we would be more inclined to say: “You must know whether you know it or not!” – On the other hand: if he had been asked, “Do you know what ‘clean’ and ‘unclean’ mean in the Jewish dietary laws?” – would it be nonsensical to say, “Let’s see – this and that are called clean; but whether _that_ is also called clean, I don’t know, etc., etc.” One would then also say, “To a certain extent, I have some understanding of the word.” Or the question would be: “Do you understand the word ‘integral’?” – But can’t you also think of cases in which it would be quite natural to say: “Yes, to a certain extent I understand the word ‘tree’; but to what extent, that remains to be seen”? And couldn’t it also happen that you _imagine_ you understand a word (no differently than understanding a method of calculation) & then realize that you haven’t understood it? “I thought I knew what ‘relative’ & ‘absolute motion’ meant, but I see that I don’t.”

### <span class="fac">[93\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/93.webp),[94\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/94.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> I imagine playing this game: A list of words of various kinds is compiled; some are very simple nouns, such as “tree,” “bread,” then, for example, also “thing,” rarer ones like “dynamometer,” “continuity,” and other specific color names such as: “sepia,” “ultramarine,” “cobalt blue”; the words “perhaps,” “about,” “the”; also words from foreign languages, more or less unusual; & also senseless sequences of sounds. These words are read aloud to me, and after each one I am to say whether I understand them or not; & to examine what was going on inside me when I had understanding or did not have it. – To the word “tree,” I will answer “yes” without hesitation—an image _may_ or may not come to mind; to a sequence of sounds I have never heard before, I will answer “no” just as readily; for words with a specific designation of color, the answer is often preceded by a mental visualization; for rarer words like “dynamometer,” a moment of thought; for words like the article “das,” perhaps a shrug; I will _sometimes_ translate words from a foreign language into German; if images come to mind, they are sometimes those of the objects the word designates (again, a thousand different cases), sometimes _other_ images. This game could be supplemented by one in which the names of _activities_ are stated & for each one asked: “Can you do that?” – The subject should then explain the reasons for answering the question one way or the other.

### <span class="fac">[95\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/95.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> It is interesting that the images that come to mind when we hear and “have understanding” of an isolated word—for example, when our comprehension is being tested—usually fail to appear at all when we read a sentence containing that word with _understanding_. The image that this evokes by no means consists of all those partial images.

### <span class="fac">[95\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/95.webp),[96\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/96.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> I once wrote: “If I am asked ‘Do you see a sphere there?’, and another time ‘Do you see the hemisphere there?’, what I see may be the same in both cases, & when I answer ‘Yes, I see it’, I nevertheless distinguish between the two hypotheses. Just as in a game of chess I distinguish between a pawn and the king, even if the current move is one that both could make, and even if a king were to function as a pawn.” In philosophy, one is always in danger of creating a myth of symbolism, or of mental processes. Instead of simply saying what everyone knows and must admit.

### <span class="fac">[96\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/96.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> One says: – “At that moment I understood what facial expression he meant – I saw it before me.” Here, then, understanding is a state of consciousness, or a process. Quite similarly: “When I heard this quartet the other day, I _understood_ it.” No; one says “I listened to it with understanding,” but not “I understood it the whole time.”

### <span class="fac">[96\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/96.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “As long as the temperature of the bar does not drop below …, one can forge it.” There is therefore sense in saying: “I can forge it from 5 to 6 o’clock.” Or: “I can play chess from 5 to 6,” i.e., for example, I have time from 5 to 6. – “As long as my pulse doesn’t drop below …, I can do this multiplication.” This multiplication takes 1½ minutes; how long does it take to _be able to_ do it? And if you _can_ do it for an hour, the whole time_,_ _do_ you start over from scratch every time?

### <span class="fac">[97\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/97.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> “I understood his facial expressions.” – – For how long? – Well, while I was watching him! Here one can say: “I _followed_ him with understanding.” What does this following consist of? _Describe_ such cases; then you will see.

### <span class="fac">[97\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/97.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> _When_ does my understanding of the sentence “Our life lasts seventy years” begin? Always? Or while I am reading it? With each word, only that word, or the whole thing at once at the end?

### <span class="fac">[97\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/97.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> It is like asking: When _can_ you play chess? Always? Or during the game? And during every move, the whole game? – And how strange that knowing how to play chess takes such a short time, while a game takes so much longer?

### <span class="fac">[97\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/97.webp),[98\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/98.webp),[99\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/99.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">4</span> It is a very curious fact that, when using language, I do not remember how I learned it. I say: “the glass is in the cupboard”; I do not know how I learned the meaning of “glass” & “cupboard.” My application of words is completely detached from explanations that were once given to me. It is as if I had coined the words myself. And here we are led back to the question: If the grammatical rules governing a word determine its meaning, must I have all these rules in my head for the word to mean anything to me? Or is it here like in a mechanism: the wheel that stands still, the wheel in _a_ position, knows, as it were, not what movement is still permitted to it, the piston does not know what law governs its movement; & yet the wheel & the piston act only through that constraint. Should I therefore say: The grammatical rules act in time? (Like that guidance.) So: The word “glass” functions only in the manner of its application. And one might conceivably ask the strange question: “How can I then know immediately what I mean by ‘glass,’ since I cannot possibly have the entire range of its applications in my head at once?” – And is it not similar in a game? One might say that I have the knowledge of the rules of the game, ‘have them in my head,’ when I play. But isn’t this “having them in my head” really just a hypothesis? Do I not have them in my head only insofar as I apply them in each particular case? – Certainly, this knowledge is merely the hypothetical reservoir from which the water actually seen flows. The understanding of language—quasi of the game—seems like a backdrop against which the individual sentence first gains meaning.

### <span class="fac">[99\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/99.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> How strange: it seems as though a physical (mechanical) guide might fail, allowing for the unforeseen, but a rule cannot! It would be, so to speak, the only reliable guide. But in what does it consist that a guide does not allow a movement, & in what that a rule does not allow it? – How does one know one thing, & how the other?

### <span class="fac">[100\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/100.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Can I (not) say: I mean the negation which, when doubled, yields an affirmation? When you spoke of red, did you mean that which one can say is bright, but not that it is green, even if you did not think of this rule nor use it? Did you _not_ use those three that constitute a negation; even if you did not use this rule? Is it a hypothesis that it was not _so_? Can it be doubtful whether it was the same thing, & be confirmed by experience?

### <span class="fac">[100\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/100.webp),[101\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/101.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">100</span> What constitutes the intention to play a game of chess? How does it differ from the intention to play another game? Chess is, after all, defined by its rules; if any of the rules were different, it would be a different game. So how do the rules of chess enter into the intention to play chess? Or do we perhaps not know which game we want to play; does that only become clear to us once we have already played it? (People say: “Surely I will know what I wanted to play!”, “Surely I will know what I wish for!”, “Surely I will know why I did it!”) Consider a justification such as: “I know that I had the intention, _because_ I thought to myself: ‘Now I’m finally going to play chess.’” It would be entirely consistent with this intention if, on the first move, I realized that I had already forgotten all the rules.

### <span class="fac">[101\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/101.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> It bothers us, as it were, that the thought of a sentence is never fully present at any given moment. We see it as an object that we create but never fully possess, for as soon as one part arises, another disappears.

### <span class="fac">[101\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/101.webp),[102\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/102.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> ‘Intuitive thinking.’ Mozart writes in a letter that a piece stands fully before his mind in an instant. – If someone were to say to me: “So it is _indeed_ possible that an entire thought is grasped and conceived _at once_”—so it is indeed possible _to call_ something ‘intuitive thinking.’ But does this phrase already provide an explanation of _what_ we mean by it? Mozart’s words give me cause for various thoughts: for example, that he certainly does _not_ mean that he hears the entire piece in an instant in his inner ear, as if all the notes of the piece were struck simultaneously or as if the piece were played through at breakneck speed; I can surmise this and that, and I get no further.

### <span class="fac">[102\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/102.webp),[103\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/103.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Understanding a sentence in the language is much more akin to understanding a theme in music (or a musical piece) than one might suppose. But what I mean is this: that understanding a linguistic sentence is closer than one thinks to what is usually called understanding a piece of music. Why do I want to bring the change in dynamics and tempo precisely to this rhythm, why draw precisely _this_ line? One might say: “because I know what it all means.” But what does it mean? I wouldn’t know how to say. To ‘give an explanation’ of it, I could only _compare_ it to something else that has the same rhythm (I mean, the same line). (One might say: “Don’t you see: it’s as if a conclusion were being drawn,” or: “it is, as it were, a parenthesis,” etc. How does one justify such comparisons? There are _various_ justifications.)

### <span class="fac">[103\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/103.webp),[104\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/104.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">11</span> We sometimes call “thinking” the mental process that accompanies a sentence, but we do not call that accompaniment a “thought.” – Speak a sentence & think it; speak it with understanding! – And now do not speak it, & do only that with which you accompanied it while speaking with understanding! – (Sing this song with expression – & now do not sing it, but repeat the expression! – And one _could_ also repeat something here: e.g., bodily vibrations, slower & faster breathing, mental images. –)

### <span class="fac">[104\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/104.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> “He said these words, but he didn’t mean anything by them.” – “Yes, – I meant something!” – “And what, precisely?” – “What I said.”

### <span class="fac">[104\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/104.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “This sentence has sense.” – “Which sense?” Compare this with: “This sequence of words is a sentence.” – “Which sentence?”

### <span class="fac">[104\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/104.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> “I had something specific in mind when I said, …” – And did you have something different in mind with each word, or the same thing throughout the entire sentence?

### <span class="fac">[104\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/104.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">4</span> By the way, it’s strange that a sequence of words can be nonsense, when one does indeed have something in mind with each word.

### <span class="fac">[105\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/105.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> What does it mean when I say that in the sentence “the rose is red,” the “is” has a different meaning than in the sentence “2 times 2 is 4”? If I wanted to say that different rules apply to these two words, the answer would be that we are talking about only _one_ word. But I can say: In the first sentence, you may replace “is” with “has the property” (ε), in the second “is” with “is equal to” ( = ), but not the other way around. And in the further course of the argument, one does something quite different with “ε” than with “ = ”.

### <span class="fac">[105\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/105.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> Can I now describe what the rules of word application say in a different way, namely by describing the process that takes place when understanding the word? I once thought that grammatical rules were the analysis of what I experience all at once when using a word. So to speak, consequences, expressions of the properties that I experience all at once when understanding.

### <span class="fac">[106\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/106.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> One might be tempted to say: negation has the property of yielding an affirmation when doubled. “Double negation yields an affirmation”—that sounds like: “Carbon and oxygen yield carbonic acid.” But in reality, double negation _does not yield_ anything; rather, it _is_ something.

### <span class="fac">[106\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/106.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> One can say: this body has the property of being brown. Brown has the property of being the color of this body. 3 has the property of being the number of my sisters (but not: 3 has the property of being half of 6. This belongs to the grammar of the sign “3”). Negation has the property of yielding truth in this sentence.

### <span class="fac">[106\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/106.webp),[107\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/107.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> “That three negations again yield a negation must already lie in the one negation that I now use.” (The temptation to invent a myth to explain the meaning.)

<span class="series-number">1</span> Not: “The cube has the property of possessing all equal sides” – but a wooden block happens to have this property, and is thereby a cube.

### <span class="fac">[107\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/107.webp)</span>

(Like: “three such horses can move this carriage.”) But in the sentence “~~~p = ~p,” there is no mention of negation at all. (The sentence “it is not raining,” for example, _deals_ with negation.) The proposition of logic is a rule for the use of the symbol “~.” One could also say: in it, the symbol “~” _has_ no meaning, but _is given_ one.

### <span class="fac">[107\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/107.webp),[108\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/108.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> It appears as though it follows from the nature of negation that ~~ p is p. (And there is _some_ truth to this. What? _Our_ nature is connected to both.)

### <span class="fac">[108\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/108.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> I would like to use the following image: that the word “is,” when it means “= ” in one instance and “ε” in another, has a different **body** of meaning behind it; that it is the same front surface that (each time) belongs to a different body; as when I see a triangle in the foreground that is, in one instance, the end face of a prism, and in another, of a tetrahedron.

### <span class="fac">[108\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/108.webp),[109\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/109.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> Or let us imagine this case: we have perfectly transparent glass cubes, one of whose side surfaces is painted red. If we arrange them next to each other, only certain arrangements of red squares will be possible in space, due to the cube shape of the glass bodies. I could now give the rule according to which red squares can be arranged in such a way that the cubes are not explicitly mentioned in it, but the essence of the cube shape would nevertheless be contained in this rule.

### <span class="fac">[109\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/109.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> But when we _see_ a cube, are we thereby truly given all the laws of its possible composition?—That is, its entire geometry? Can I deduce the geometry of the cube from a cube?

### <span class="fac">[109\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/109.webp),[110\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/110.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> The cube is then a notation of the rule. – And if we had found such a rule, we really could not note it better than by drawing a cube (& the fact that a _drawing_ suffices here is very significant). But the cube or the drawing of the cube are, after all, only signs; they function only as signs insofar as I now _use_ them in a system of connections with other signs. When I think that the cube already contains the entire geometry of the cube—which cube? The cube with faces, or an iron cube? Or is there an ideal geometric cube?—obviously, the process comes to mind when we derive geometric theorems from a drawing, a model, or an idea. But what role does the model play in this? That of a sign, with a specific mode of use. It is, however, (interesting &) curious how we use a figure (the image of the cube, for example) again & again, with ever-changing elements. And it is this sign (_with the identity of a sign_) that we take for that cube in which the geometric laws already lie.

### <span class="fac">[110\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/110.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> The sentence “It is raining” says something about the weather, but not about what I am saying. How then can “It is raining” say the same thing as: “The sentence ‘it is raining’ is true,” since _that_ says something about the words? Can it say the same thing, if one time I answer the question about the weather: “It is raining,” and another time: “I am truthfully telling you: ‘it is raining’.”

### <span class="fac">[111\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/111.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> The _explanation_ of signs leads us from these signs to others. If you think that language, its essence, must necessarily be explainable, then think that the explanation is given in a language.

### <span class="fac">[111\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/111.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> Imagine a sentence of word-language, such as: “I am smaller than you,” replaced by signs of sign language. Do we still feel the same need for _explanation_ here, as with the words?

### <span class="fac">[111\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/111.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Sign language was not taught to us deliberately; and certainly not through explanations of signs.

### <span class="fac">[111\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/111.webp),[112\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/112.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">4</span> Is it the case that an explanation, a table for example, can first be used in such a way that one ‘looks it up,’ that one then, as it were, looks it up in one’s head (calls it to mind, or the like); and that finally one works without this table, i.e., as if it had never existed? In this latter case, one is (therefore) obviously playing a different game; the table has been eliminated from the game. I may sometimes refer to it, but then I do what the blind person does, who perhaps refers to the sense of touch. An explanation, a table, becomes history when we no longer use it.

### <span class="fac">[112\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/112.webp),[113\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/113.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> I must distinguish between the cases: when I once follow a table, and another time act in accordance with the table (the rule), without using the table. – The rule whose learning led me to act in this way now is, as a cause of my way of acting, history. – However, insofar as it is a general description of our way of acting, it is a hypothesis. (The hypothesis, for example, that these two people sitting at the chessboard will act according to the given laws.) But the players can also use the rule in the game by looking it up before each move (this can be part of the game). Here, the use of the rule is a move in the game. “Here, however, there is a difficulty; the player who plays without using the rules, yes, who has never seen a list of the rules, could nevertheless, if asked, draw one up; and I do not mean – behavioristically – by repeatedly observing how he acts in these cases, but by, standing before a move, saying: ‘in this case one moves _so_’.” – But if this is the case, then it only shows that he will state rules under certain circumstances; not that he made use of them when making his move.

### <span class="fac">[113\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/113.webp),[114\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/114.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> It is possible that someone forgets the meaning of the word “blue.” What has he forgotten? “I no longer know what ‘blue’ means.” – Is this a specific state of mind? – But there are different states of mind under different circumstances to which this sentence may correspond. Consider: “The one who has forgotten the meaning of the word ‘blue’ and was asked to select a blue object from others, feels when looking at these objects that the connection between the word ‘blue’ and those colors no longer exists (is broken); and the connection is restored when we repeat the explanation of the word to him.”

### <span class="fac">[114\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/114.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> How can one, to test one’s understanding of the word “blue,” conjure up a mental image of blue? For how can the word “blue” show me which color from the palette of my imagination I am to choose, and how can the color that comes to mind show me that _it_ is the right one? _Do_ I therefore _choose_ an image that fits the word “blue”?—And might not the wrong image arise? _And how does that become apparent_?

### <span class="fac">[115\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/115.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> What is the criterion for my understanding of the word “blue”: that this color comes to mind (that I instinctively direct my gaze toward _it_, and the like); or that other people agree with my use of the word? <s> Suppose that whenever I hear the word “blue,” a certain color has a degree of regularity in that it regularly comes to mind (according to the normal use of words: “not always the same”); could I not say: “I call ‘blue’ the color that comes to mind when I hear the word”?

</s> But why am I talking about mental images?! When asked to point to something blue, I point to an object of a certain color, & if this—judged by some other criteria—is always the same when the word “blue” is used, then saying that an _image_ comes to mind when I hear the word & that I orient myself by _it_ is no _explanation_ for this.

### <span class="fac">[115\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/115.webp),[116\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/116.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Suppose tomorrow I call “blue” what I have always called that—that is, that is what _I_ say; but everyone else I meet says: “That’s red!”—who is right then? If I verify that I understand a word by calling an image to mind or pointing to an object, this is a criterion of _correct_ understanding only insofar as it enables me to communicate with others.

### <span class="fac">[116\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/116.webp),[117\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/117.webp),[118\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/118.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> But couldn’t one distinguish here between subjective understanding of the word and objective understanding? For instance, I read about a blue pot, and an image immediately springs to mind with certainty. (As if a button had been pressed and a small panel with the image on it were connected to the button.) With the word “Puhu,” such an image does not spring to mind. This is _a_ difference. But whether I have _correctly_ understood the words “blue pot” in accordance with general use is not yet settled. To have a subjectivist understanding of the word would thus mean—to put it simply: it is associated with an image. To have an objective sense of the word would mean: it is associated with the _correct_ image. – One could therefore also say: Language, insofar as one understands it only subjectively, is indeed not a means of communication with others, but a tool for the individual’s _private_ use. The question, however, is: whether we would still call this utterance of sound combinations, writing of lines, and the like, ‘language,’ and whether we would still call it a _‘tool.’_ For he would now have to play language-games with himself—and he can _certainly_ do that: imagine a Robinson Crusoe who uses a language (signs) for his private use; imagine you were seeing him do so (without his knowing it); You would thus see how, on numerous occasions, he carves lines into wood and utters sounds: would you under _any_ circumstances call this ‘the use of signs’? Yet only if _you_ were to observe a certain regularity in the process. We observe a person who, on various occasions—without any regularity—emits certain sounds—now we say: “This may be a purely private language; he probably imagines the same thing every time he makes the same sound.” –

### <span class="fac">[118\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/118.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> “Once you know _what_ the word denotes, you have understanding of it, you know its full application.”

### <span class="fac">[118\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/118.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> Imagine that a person used “yellow” to perform the designation of every yellow object (much like we use “something yellow”). He would then always give the correct ostensive explanation, yet his use of the word would not be ours.

### <span class="fac">[118\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/118.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Forgetting the meaning of a word—remembering it again. What variations are there? What does one recall, what comes to mind when one remembers again what the English word “perhaps” means?—How does something _like_ _this_ happen: I say, “Now, for the first time, I know what the words mean: ‘the blue ether’”?

### <span class="fac">[119\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/119.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Is it correct to say: “The word ‘red’ is not a sign in itself, but requires a supplement in the memory of colors”?

### <span class="fac">[119\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/119.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> If the patient rings the bell, I come to him. Do I obey the command of the ringing by first forming a mental image of the action he is asking me to perform? But I could probably say: “Now do what you did yesterday on this occasion, according to your memory” – and if he remembers, he can obey the command. If not, it is as if I had commanded: “Do what is written on this piece of paper!” – and the piece of paper is blank. Or it says: “Buy n plums & <math class="stacked" display="inline"><msup><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mspace width="0.3em"/><mo>+</mo><mspace width="0.3em"/><mtext>2n</mtext><mspace width="0.3em"/><mo>+</mo><mspace width="0.3em"/><mn>2</mn><mspace width="0.3em"/><mo>=</mo><mspace width="0.3em"/><mn>0</mn></math>”.

### <span class="fac">[119\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/119.webp),[120\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/120.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Is my memory necessarily more reliable than a record, my memory of colors, for example, than a color sample? On different occasions, I will check one by the other; and so on. Memory is not always the final authority. Could I not, under certain circumstances, say: “My memory always fades a bit”?

### <span class="fac">[120\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/120.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> How can I _justify_ forming this mental image based on these words? Did someone show me the image of the color blue and tell me that _this_ is it? What does “_this_ mental image” mean here? Can I point to it? Can I point to it _within myself_, if it is _my_ mental image? If I imagine a blue circle and an arrow pointing at it—does the arrow point to my image? Could I give myself _such_ private, ostensive definitions? (Always think of the _use_ of signs!) (All this is connected to the problem: whether, and how, I can know whether, and what, the other person feels, sees, etc.)

### <span class="fac">[121\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/121.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> I see someone & remember him. Does a ‘memory-image’ of the person always (or even just _often_) come to mind?

### <span class="fac">[121\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/121.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> Is sign language therefore incapable of explanation? – Certainly; for example, through words.

### <span class="fac">[121\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/121.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Think of reading the written word aloud, or of writing down what is spoken. We _could_ imagine a kind of table (here a mechanism) that one could use as a guide for the translation. But we do not use any such guide. No act of memory, nothing, mediates between the written sign and the sound.

### <span class="fac">[121\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/121.webp),[122\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/122.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">4</span> It is _one_ thing to select objects from among others using a color sample, another to do so using a memory-image of a color, and yet another to act immediately upon a word. “But the color can _guide_ me in my choice, and the word cannot!”—But would you not say that you are guided by the written text when reading, and by the dictation when writing? Yes, I do not want to say that this case is of the same kind as the previous one; but there is an analogy, and a dissimilarity. A pattern also guides me, insofar as _I_ allow myself to be guided by it. – Imagine various borderline cases in which it is difficult to say whether one should call this “being guided by the signs” or not.

### <span class="fac">[122\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/122.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> As with all metaphysics, the ‘harmony between thought and reality’ is to be found in the grammar of language.

### [PG I App IV](/w-pg-1-app-4/#ms-116-122.3+123.1) <span class="fac">[122\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/122.webp),[123\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/123.webp)</span> {#12231231}

<span class="series-number">2</span> What can lead us to believe that there is a kind of correspondence between thought and reality? – Instead of “correspondence,” one could easily say (here): “pictoriality.” But is pictoriality a correspondence? In the Logical-Philosophical Treatise, I said something like: it is a correspondence of form.  <s>But that is a mistake. Everything can be an image of everything—if we correspond the concept of the image accordingly. What I said now amounts to this: every projection, by whatever method, must have something in common with the projected. That is, I expand the concept of “having in common” and make it equivalent to the general concept of projection. A certain kind of generalization thus imposes itself on me, a form of representation, a certain aspect. </s>

### [PG I App IV](/w-pg-1-app-4/#ms-116-123.2) <span class="fac">[123\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/123.webp)</span> {#1232}

<span class="series-number">1</span> Above all, “image” is used ambiguously here. One wants to say: a command is an image of the action that was carried out according to it; but also, an image of the action that _is_ _to_ be carried out according to it.

### [PG I App IV](/w-pg-1-app-4/#ms-116-124.1+125.1) <span class="fac">[124\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/124.webp),[125\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/125.webp)</span> {#12411251}

<span class="series-number">1</span> One might say: a working drawing _serves as an image_ of the object that the worker is to produce according to it. And one could call the “projection method” here the manner in which the worker must translate such a drawing into the work. One could now make the following expression: the projection method mediates between the drawing and the object; it extends from the drawing to the workpiece. One thus makes a comparison between the projection method and projection lines that extend from one figure to another. – But if the projection method is a bridge, then it is one that is not built until the application is made. – The comparison, however, leads to the idea that the image, _together with_ the projection rays, does not merely permit various types of _application_, but that through this the depicted object, even if it does not actually exist, is ethically determined—namely, determined as _if_ it _were_ present. (It is “determined to be both yes and no.”) One can then call the working drawing_, with_ the method of its application, an “image.” And under this method, one now imagines something that follows the working drawing, even if it is not used. (One can ‘_describe_’ an application, even if it does not exist.)

### [PG I App IV](/w-pg-1-app-4/#ms-116-125.2+126.1) <span class="fac">[125\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/125.webp),[126\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/126.webp)</span> {#12521261}

<span class="series-number">1</span> I now ask: “How could the working drawing be used as a representation if there were not already a correspondence with what is to be made?”—But what does that mean? Well, something like this: How could I play the piano from sheet music if it did not already have some kind of relationship to hand movements of a certain sort? And this relationship consists, of course, _sometimes_ in a certain correspondence (similarity), but sometimes not in a correspondence, but only in the fact that we have learned to apply the signs in such and such a way. But to make all these cases the same—for _that_ is what tempts us—serves the confusion between the projection rays that connect the image with the object, and the projection method. One might say: I still count the projection rays as part of the image—but not the projection method. One could, of course, also say: I still count a _description_ of the projection method as part of the image. I therefore imagine that the difference between proposition and reality is balanced out by the projection rays, which belong to the image, to the thought, and which leave no room for a method of application. There is (rather) only agreement and disagreement.

### <span class="fac">[126\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/126.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> “The possibility of agreement already presupposes _an_ agreement.” – I think someone said: “Being able to play chess is a kind of playing chess.”

### <span class="fac">[126\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/126.webp),[127\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/127.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “A false sentence only represents reality _incorrectly_, but it does represent _reality_.” A false description of my room still describes something comparable to my room. This comes down to this: two different lengths at least have in common that they are lengths; two shapes, that they are shapes; etc. – If this is not to be utter nonsense, then it would be something like a grammatical statement about the similarity in the application of expressions of certain classes.

### <span class="fac">[127\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/127.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> “The painted likeness, apart from the similarity, agrees with the person in that it exhibits colors.” One could call this an agreement, and this is lacking between a person and a charcoal drawing.

### <span class="fac">[127\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/127.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “Red and green agree in that they are colors.” – Here one can say: “What if they did not agree?”

### <span class="fac">[127\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/127.webp),[128\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/128.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> This remark is important, because we always want to explain the oddity of thinking, the consequence of grammatical misunderstandings, through its psychological, elusive, character.

### <span class="fac">[128\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/128.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> One wants to give reasons, and reasons, and reasons. With the feeling: where there is a reason, everything is in order. If no reason is present, then the matter is irrational, and therefore not interesting to us. (The use of “irrational” is similar to that of the word “fall” in the sentence: “If the earth were not held in some way, it would have to fall.”) We do not simply want to describe what happens, but we always want to (only) explain.

### <span class="fac">[128\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/128.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> “Ideally, the chain of reasons would extend into infinity.” – Yes, what is the function, the purpose, of a reason?

### <span class="fac">[128\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/128.webp),[129\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/129.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> What if we ask someone: “How do you know that your description reproduces what you see?” and he answers: “I _mean_ that with these words.” – It is about a landscape. What is this ‘_that_’? What makes a series of words a description of what I see in front of me? That I _mean_ what I see in front of me with them? _How does one do that?_ – Suppose I say: “a b c d,” and mean by it: the weather is nice – namely, when uttering those signs, I have the experience which I would otherwise only have if I used “a” year in, year out in the sense of “that,” “b” in the sense of “weather,” etc. – does “a b c d” then mean: the weather is nice?

### <span class="fac">[129\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/129.webp),[130\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/130.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Can I not mean what I want with words? – Look at the door of your room, say a series of arbitrary sound combinations, and _mean_ by them a description of this door! –

### <span class="fac">[130\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/130.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Suppose I say: “a b c d” and mean by it: the weather is nice – namely, I have when uttering those signs the same experience which one would otherwise only have if one used “a” year in, year out in the sense of “that,” “b” in the sense of “weather,” etc. – Does “a b c d” then mean: the weather is nice?

### <span class="fac">[130\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/130.webp),[131\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/131.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> And yet there are differences in the experience of a sentence. Try this: in a conversation with someone who, let us say, speaks German, but, as you _know_, does not understand English, – you suddenly say to him an _English_ sentence. You are talking about a trip you want to take, and now you say to him the sentence, that you do not want to go if it rains, in the foreign language that you master, but he does not. – You would notice that the English sentence, as it were, runs empty within you, that you cannot ‘mean’ it in this conversation; as you would if you used it in an English conversation.

### <span class="fac">[131\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/131.webp),[132\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/132.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Imagine someone pointing to his cheek with the gesture for toothache and saying, “abracadabra!” – We ask, “What do you mean?” and he answers, “I meant ‘toothache’.” – You immediately think to yourself: how can one—what does it even mean to _“mean_ ‘toothache’” with that word? And yet, in a different context, you would have asserted that the act of _meaning_ this or that is precisely the most important thing in the use of language. But how—can’t I say: “By ‘abracadabra’ I mean toothache”? Of course; but that is a definition, not a description of what is going on inside me when I utter the word. And one can also say: “When I said ‘the Führer,’ I meant Adolf Hitler”—and I suppose that when I said those words, an image of the man had indeed come to mind;—and _yet_ the sentence: “When I said … I meant …” does not say: this or that was going on inside me when I spoke the words. “By the word … I meant …” does not mean the same thing as: when I said that word, I thought of … One can certainly think of pain when uttering the word “abracadabra”; but one does not express that with the words: “I meant … by the word …”. Rather, this statement is always an expression of a _rule_.

### <span class="fac">[132\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/132.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> One could distinguish between a ‘surface grammar’ and a ‘deep grammar’ in the use of a word. What immediately strikes us about the use of a word is its function in _sentence structure_—the part of its use, one might say, that can be grasped by the _ear_. – And now perform a comparison of the facts of the deep grammar of the word “mean” (for example) with what its surface grammar would lead us to expect. No wonder it is difficult to make sense of it.

### <span class="fac">[133\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/133.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Grammar does not say how language must be constructed in order to fulfil its purpose, to have such and such an effect on people. It merely describes, but in no way provides an explanation for the use of signs.

### <span class="fac">[133\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/133.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> The concept of a living being has the same indeterminacy as that of language.

### <span class="fac">[133\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/133.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> “The purpose of language is to express thoughts.” – So it is probably the purpose of every sentence to express a thought; – what thought, then, does the sentence “It is raining” express, for example? – – Not: “Without language, we could not communicate with one another.” But rather: without language, we humans cannot influence things in such and such a way; we cannot build roads and machines; etc. And also: Without the use of speech and writing, people could not communicate with one another.

### <span class="fac">[134\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/134.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Comparison: Inventing a game – inventing a language – inventing a machine.

### <span class="fac">[134\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/134.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> Are the rules of a game arbitrary? – I could call them “arbitrary” to distinguish them, for example, from the rules of fingering, if the point is to be made that this or that is not immediately conducive or detrimental to the game’s actions. Although a rule can make a game interesting, boring, or fun.

### <span class="fac">[134\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/134.webp),[135\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/135.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> One can call the rules of grammar “arbitrary” if the intention is to say that the _purpose_ of grammar is solely that of language. And it would be nonsensical to say, for example: language must contain nouns, adjectives, verbs, and numerals because there are things, properties, activities, and numbers, and the like. As if the case were comparable to this: Astronomy must speak of 4 moons of Jupiter because there are 4 moons of Jupiter.

### <span class="fac">[135\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/135.webp),[136\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/136.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">4</span> Let us imagine a diary kept with the aid of a number, an independent system of punctuation. Each page bears a date & is, as it were, divided into 24 sections like a timetable; & now “A” in our language means: I go to sleep; “B”: I get up; “C”: I eat; etc. How does he know that what he notes with “A” is always the same? He consults his memory. But that does not get us anywhere. The statement of memory is then simply added to the sign. (Imagine that instead of memory, a die served him, & he rolled (now) what he was to write.) <s> What use could such a diary be to him? Perhaps as a source of pleasurable memories. He reads it later & accompanies the reading with images; & sometimes he remembers that it really was so. </s> But doesn't that raise the question: On what grounds did I say above: “A” means in our language: I go to sleep, etc. etc.? Only that made it possible to call these signs “diary entries”! So ask yourself: from what could I conclude that these signs mean this & that? Suppose these signs were his whole language & our interpretation was based on the fact that he regularly records a sign on certain occasions. – What use could the diary be to him now? – We suppose, for example, that it is to allow him, when he reads it again, to indulge in memories. – Should I then say that a diary page tells him something? & in what does it consist that it tells him something? In the experience of remembering that he has when reading. – But if he had the same experience when looking at a series of trees, – would we say that they tell him something?

### <span class="fac">[136\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/136.webp),[137\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/137.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> When one thinks of “experience of remembering”, one thinks (naturally) primarily of something like – a memory-image. Of course, there are memory-images – I can easily conjure some up in my mind. But how do I conjure it up in my mind, in what environment of thoughts? And if I now consider it in isolation, fix it, is _it_ itself the memory? I say, for example: “I see myself walking with a friend there & there.” But – how do I know that _I_ am the one & that it is my friend? Are the portraits so accurately depicted? Of course not. But I _say_ that it is me with my friend; I make this transition from the image (from the imagination) to words, or from this image to certain other images; etc.

### <span class="fac">[137\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/137.webp),[138\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/138.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> And if I say: “I see a memory-image before me” – how do you know what I am experiencing? You can’t – as they say – look inside me (only I can). So I am mainly making a communication to myself, or should I say: I am only making a real communication to myself, not to another. But how does it communicate something to _me_, by uttering sounds during or after an experience (for example, the sentence: “I have an experience of remembering”)?

### <span class="fac">[138\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/138.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">1</span> Or should I have said to myself, instead of “I have an experience of remembering”: “I have _that_”? (Or even just: “_That_.”)

### <span class="fac">[138\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/138.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">2</span> What does one usually call a “communication”? You have to think of communication games.

### <span class="fac">[138\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/138.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">3</span> Do I communicate something to myself when, looking at this paper, I say: “This paper is white”? & what does it actually mean: “to say something to oneself”? Does one say everything to oneself that one utters when no one else is present?

### <span class="fac">[138\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/138.webp),[139\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/139.webp)</span>

But can one not remind oneself, give oneself commands, yes, even ask and answer oneself? Oh yes – one can also play chess against oneself, yes, perhaps even win money from oneself – if one calls it that.
