### <span class="fac">[290\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/290.webp)</span>

Our mistake is to look for an explanation where we should see the facts as ‘proto-phenomena.’ That is, where we should say: _this language-game is being played_.

### <span class="fac">[290\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/290.webp)</span>

It is not about the explanation of the language-game through feelings, but about the ascertainment of the language-game.

### <span class="fac">[290\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/290.webp),[291\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/291.webp)</span>

_Why_ would I tell someone that I once had such-and-such a wish? See the language-game as the _primary thing_! And see feelings, etc., as a way of viewing the language-game! One might ask: How did humans ever come to express a past wish?

### <span class="fac">[291\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/291.webp)</span>

Let us imagine that this utterance always takes the form: “I said to myself: ‘If only I could stay longer!’” The purpose of such communication could be to let the other person know my reactions. (“Vouloir dire.”)

### <span class="fac">[291\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/291.webp)</span>

(My methods have similarities with certain very general methods in mathematics.)

### <span class="fac">[291\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/291.webp)</span>

Or one could also use _the following_ _expression_: “I said to myself, as it were: ‘N should come to me.’”

### <span class="fac">[291\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/291.webp),[292\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/292.webp)</span>

If someone asks me, “Who did you just talk to about N?” and I answer, “We talked about A,” then it is clear: with this answer, I only establish a connection between our conversation and A, a connection that can be demonstrated at the time.

### <span class="fac">[292\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/292.webp)</span>

If Max says, “The Prince has guardianship over the troops,” he means Wallenstein.

### <span class="fac">[292\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/292.webp)</span>

Suppose someone said: We do not know whether Max means Wallenstein; in this sentence, he may also mean another prince.

### <span class="fac">[292\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/292.webp),[293\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/293.webp)</span>

If I am now talking with A about a person named ‘B’, but there are two people with that name, what then is the connection between our conversation and one of them? – “Well, you _are thinking_ of this person.” – But what kind of connection does that establish? – Well, one can of course easily imagine all sorts of connections between the conversation and one of the two. But isn’t it clear that a _decisive_ connection cannot be made by our thinking if it cannot also be made by the conversation itself? (The internal process cannot do anything that the external process cannot also do.)

### <span class="fac">[293\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/293.webp),[294\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/294.webp)</span>

What does it consist of, my speaking to a certain person? We are both alone in my room. We are talking about a certain object that concerns us both. I stand before him, look at him, and speak. – But I can also do that without speaking to him. And I can also speak to him without standing before him, without looking at him. Yes, even if others were in the room with us. Yes, even if I were looking at someone else! – Someone asks, “Who did you just speak to?” – I say, “I spoke to N.” I wanted him to relate my words to himself, even though I wasn't looking at him. But when I stood before him and looked at him, I also wanted him to relate my words to himself!

### <span class="fac">[294\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/294.webp)</span>

Try this experiment: Say “May the devil take him!” and have different people in mind. Even those you barely know, like the clerk in a store. – You will be imagining him while you say it. And then you say that you meant N. But you didn't say the name to yourself while you were cursing. So how do you know it was N whose image you saw? You went from the image to the name “N.” Saying that you knew earlier who you meant is similar to saying that you know how this melody continues.

### <span class="fac">[294\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/294.webp)</span>

“I meant N” seems to _report_ on a connection & also to _create_ one.

### <span class="fac">[294\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/294.webp),[295\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/295.webp)</span>

I draw a head. You ask me, “Who is this supposed to be?” – I say, “It’s supposed to be N.” – You: “It doesn’t look like him. It looks more like M.” – When I said that it was supposed to be N, was I making a connection, or was I reporting (on) one? What connection had existed?

### <span class="fac">[295\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/295.webp)</span>

It is as if I could say: “I seal a certain connection.” And don't I mean that a plausible line is carried on to its natural end?

### <span class="fac">[295\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/295.webp)</span>

What is there to say for the claim that my words describe a connection that existed? Well, they refer to various things that did not only come into being with them. They say, for example, that I would have given a certain answer if I had been asked at the time. And even if this is only conditional, it still says something about the past.

### <span class="fac">[295\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/295.webp)</span>

Who or what I meant can, for example, reveal my attitude.

### <span class="fac">[296\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/296.webp)</span>

“I did not point to the new upholstery of the armchair, but to the old wood, & spoke _of it_.” – _That_ was my attitude. “If you had asked me for it back then, I would have gladly given it to you!” – That’s how I was back then. – Is this statement of the same kind as: “If you had ordered me to lift this weight back then, I would have been too weak to do so.”?

### <span class="fac">[296\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/296.webp),[297\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/297.webp)</span>

“I saw him & waved my finger. I wanted, for a moment, for him to come to me.” Can one say: These words “I wanted …” are a description _of a human_ condition? That is, what was happening there could only be described as _a_ case of typical human behavior. When we see a sketch by an artist, we recognize the tip of a finger standing alone as such; but only because we know that this person is drawing parts of the human body. We would correctly describe the sketch as that of a fingertip.

### <span class="fac">[297\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/297.webp)</span>

In a discussion: I wanted to say this and that for a moment. I communicate this to another. What does he gather from it?

That is, probably: How can he use it?

### <span class="fac">[297\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/297.webp),[298\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/298.webp)</span>

When I recall that I wanted to say this and that for a moment, I usually recall certain ‘details’. These details are not irrelevant in the sense that other circumstances I can also recall are. But the person to whom I say “I wanted to say for a moment…” does not learn these details from this & does not have to guess them. He does not have to know, for example, that I had already opened my mouth to speak & had the image of a certain page of a book in my mind. But he _can_ ‘picture’ the process in this way.

### <span class="fac">[298\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/298.webp)</span>

If I now gather together what I remember, all of this seems insufficient as evidence that I wanted this and that. Now the question is: If I could remember having said “I want…,” would _that_ be enough evidence for me? Well, in any case, I could establish a law and say: If I said, “I want this and that,” then I wanted this and that.

### <span class="fac">[298\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/298.webp)</span>

Of course, there is a profound error here when I say that the evidence is insufficient. For I could not say _for_ _what_ it is insufficient. However, one can certainly say that I cannot _derive_ the sentence “I wanted to say: …” from the events I remember.

### <span class="fac">[299\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/299.webp)</span>

The sentence “I wanted to say…” seems to say more than what the remembering warrants. And it would also be wrong to say: What you remember is just a special case of what is called “wanting to say this and that.” As if the case were similar to this: ‘making preparations to depart’—which can consist of packing suitcases, saying goodbye to people, etc., etc.—but also in putting on a hat and walking to the train station. If I see him walking to the station, do I know that he wants to depart? Yes, I don’t know it even then, if I see him buying a ticket. And yet, one will correctly call that making preparations to depart: under certain circumstances.

### <span class="fac">[299\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/299.webp)</span>

If I wanted to say this or that back then, it _could_ be that I wrote it down at the time. If I wrote it down, it is a testimony to me later that I wanted this. If I did not write it down, the case must still have been similar to the first one.

### <span class="fac">[300\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/300.webp)</span>

It is evidently correct that I can say: under those & those circumstances, I would have said that back then. – And what can entitle me to make this assertion? I am, as it were, asserting a state of mind. But not merely as if I were saying, for example, that I _could_ have counted from 1 to 100 yesterday; rather, the state of mind was, as it were, acutely present. And the case is therefore perhaps similar to this: I _could_ have continued the sequence yesterday following your explanation, if nothing had come between us. Is there any verification for this? Under what circumstances would I _believe_ him that he could have done it? For example, if I knew that he had mastered a certain technique.

### <span class="fac">[300\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/300.webp),[301\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/301.webp)</span>

I once asked: “Could I always say, ‘It _seems to me_ as if I wanted to say that yesterday’ instead of ‘I wanted to say that yesterday’?” – But I can also say: “I _know_ I wanted to say that yesterday.” – Well, I _say_ that—and say it with conviction. It is the expression of a _kind_ of memory. Well, does it have no use?! Will people always believe the statement? Not under all circumstances. Sometimes, for example, they will reply: “You’re just imagining that.” Sometimes, however: “I know you wanted to say it; I could tell by looking at you.” The difficulty here seems to lie in the fact that this statement of memory is, so to speak, _only_ _half_ verifiable. On the one hand, there may be evidence for or against its truth; on the other hand, it does not rely on the circumstances that speak for or against it.

### <span class="fac">[301\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/301.webp)</span>

Well, the sentence is used as it is used.

### <span class="fac">[301\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/301.webp),[302\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/302.webp)</span>

The grammar of the expression “wanting to say something” is related to that of “being able to say something.” In one case, the memory of an intention; in the other, of an understanding. (A potential behavior towards a sentence.)

### <span class="fac">[302\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/302.webp)</span>

Does one learn what “wanting to say something” means by being shown which process or state is so called; as one learns what a crowd of people or a murmur is called?!

### <span class="fac">[302\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/302.webp)</span>

What is the philosophical importance of the question “How does one learn the meaning of the word …?”?

### <span class="fac">[302\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/302.webp)</span>

One can only say something if one has learned to speak: so whoever _wants_ to say something must also have learned to speak; & yet it is clear that in wanting to speak, one did not have to speak. Just as in wanting to dance, one does not dance. And when one thinks about it, the mind reaches for the _idea_ of dancing, speaking, etc.

### <span class="fac">[302\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/302.webp),[303\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/303.webp)</span>

As if wanting to dance were the same as making a _plan_ for the dance. But one can also _want_ to make a plan.

### <span class="fac">[303\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/303.webp)</span>

Or one might say: “Wanting to do something is _similar_ to devising a _plan_ for doing it.” And a plan is, of course, not _private_. One can only make a plan within a technique. And others can know just as well as I do that a plan has been made.

### <span class="fac">[303\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/303.webp)</span>

I said that I had prepared everything for the journey: my preparations consisted of the fact that I … – Can one also say: “I wanted to say at that time … – My intention to say this consisted of the fact that I …”? And from this one could infer: having an intention is a specific state of mind.

### <span class="fac">[303\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/303.webp)</span>

It is therefore a question of the applicability of “… it consists of the fact that …”.

### <span class="fac">[303\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/303.webp),[304\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/304.webp)</span>

“I prepared everything for the departure.” – “Tell me, what did you actually do? What did your preparations consist of?” – – Could one also say: “Tell me, what did your intention in saying that consist of?” In the first case, we can infer from the details, for example, whether he had really prepared everything for the trip. Can we also learn whether he _really_ wanted to say that—whether he _wanted_ it _thoroughly_, so to speak—if he tells us what image he had in mind, etc.?

### <span class="fac">[304\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/304.webp)</span>

And if I now knew the details, could I say: “If you only had this image in mind, then one can hardly call that the intention to say it”?

### <span class="fac">[304\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/304.webp),[305\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/305.webp)</span>

Imagine this case: I tell someone that I followed a certain path according to a plan that I had prepared beforehand. I show him this ‘plan’ and it consists of lines on a piece of paper; but I cannot explain in what way these lines are the plan of my walk, and I cannot tell the other person any rule according to which to read it. However, I did follow the drawing with all the characteristic signs of map reading. I could call such a drawing a ‘private’ plan; or the phenomenon I have just described: “following a private plan”. (But this expression would of course be very misleading.)

### <span class="fac">[305\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/305.webp)</span>

Could I now say: “That I wanted to dance back then, I read, as it were, from a plan, even though there is no plan”? But that means nothing other than: _I am now inclined to say_: I read the intention to dance into certain states of mind that I remember.

### <span class="fac">[305\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/305.webp),[306\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/306.webp)</span>

“I wanted to say …” – You remember various details. But they all do not show this intention. It is as if a picture of an action had been taken, but only some scattered details can be seen, here a hand, there a piece of a face, or a hat, the rest is dark. And now it is as if I knew for sure what the whole picture represents. As if I could read the darkness.

### <span class="fac">[306\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/306.webp)</span>

If you remember that you wanted to say this or that, you remember neither having said it nor any event from which one could deduce, according to any rule, _what_ you wanted to say. Your memory reaction is the words “I wanted to say …”.

### <span class="fac">[306\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/306.webp)</span>

“I wanted to say” (“I was going to say”) – So I didn’t say anything after all; & I didn’t make any other signs either. What one ‘wanted to say’ had thus _not_ been said _in any form_.

### <span class="fac">[306\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/306.webp)</span>

It is as if I were executing a sketch according to the intuition of the master.

### <span class="fac">[306\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/306.webp),[307\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/307.webp)</span>

If I say “I wanted to say…” & this statement is based on the thoughts, images, etc. to which I remember, then another person to whom I now communicate these thoughts, images, etc. must be able to infer with equal certainty that I wanted to say… back then. But he could not. Yes, if I now infer from this evidence that I wanted to say this & that, then the other person would rightly say that this inference is very uncertain.

### <span class="fac">[307\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/307.webp)</span>

It would sound odd if I said that the one who is certain that he wanted to say this & that is certain of a _picture_.

### <span class="fac">[307\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/307.webp)</span>

Can one say: Whoever says that he wanted to do (or say) this & that, he is giving a picture of himself.

### <span class="fac">[307\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/307.webp)</span>

I am convinced of a _picture_. But how is that possible?

### <span class="fac">[307\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/307.webp),[308\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/308.webp)</span>

But if I really wanted to say that back then—isn’t it enough to communicate to the other person what I actually did back then? Imagine I’m waiting for someone at the train station; I see him coming from a distance and rush toward him with a joyful face. And imagine this process was interrupted by a sudden event after the first step. Then I would still say that I wanted to rush toward him.

### <span class="fac">[308\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/308.webp)</span>

You have done, thought, felt what you have done, thought, felt. – But is that not enough; must I still add something? But do I add something when I say that you wanted to go towards him? Don’t _you_ want to say that you would have done this if nothing had intervened?

### <span class="fac">[308\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/308.webp)</span>

So, you do want to say more than just report what you have done, thought & felt? And why?

### <span class="fac">[309\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/309.webp)</span>

Do I plant the seed & let it grow?

### <span class="fac">[309\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/309.webp)</span>

I want to say: I don’t just know what I did back then, but also what it would have unfolded into. Yes, I know this as certainly as the first. Because that is the _opinion_ that I had.

### <span class="fac">[309\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/309.webp)</span>

It is like when I say: I know for sure how I would have continued the melody if I had not been interrupted. Or is it not so?

### <span class="fac">[309\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/309.webp),[310\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/310.webp)</span>

Why do I want to communicate to him not only what I did, but also an intention? – Not because the intention was also something that happened back then. But because I want to communicate something about _myself_, which goes beyond what happened back then. I reveal my inner being to him when I say what I wanted to do. – But not on the basis of self-observation, but through a reaction (one could also call it an intuition).

### <span class="fac">[310\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/310.webp)</span>

If I had _visions_ concerning my mental life in the past, then these could be of great importance.

### <span class="fac">[310\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/310.webp)</span>

Can I use the word “bububu” to mean: “If it doesn’t rain, I will go for a walk”? – Only in a language can I mean something by something. (This clearly shows that the grammar of “meaning” is not similar to that of “imagining something” & the like.)

### <span class="fac">[310\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/310.webp),[311\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/311.webp)</span>

“I am thinking of N.” “I am talking about N.” How do I talk about N? I say something like “I have to visit N today.” – But that is not enough! With N, I could mean different people who have that name. “So there must be another connection between my speech and that N, because otherwise I didn’t _really_ mean _him_.” Certainly, such a connection exists, only not as you imagine it: namely through a mental (ethereal) mechanism. (One compares “meaning him” with “shooting him”.)

### <span class="fac">[311\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/311.webp),[312\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/312.webp)</span>

If I allude to N. with a remark, this may—under certain circumstances—be discernible from my gaze, facial expression, etc. And if I also share my feelings, ideas, etc., with someone during my allusion, these may complete the typical picture of the allusion (or such _a_ picture). But it does not follow that the expression “to allude to N.” has the meaning: behaviour in this way, feeling this, imagining this, etc. And here some will say: “Of course not! We have always seen that. And there must simply be a common thread running through all these phenomena, which, as it were, wrap around this thread & make it hard to find.” And that is not true either.

But it would also be wrong to say that “allude” is used as a designation for a family of mental and other processes. For one does not ask: “How did you allude to him? Was it with a facial expression, a gesture, a thought?”—as one might well ask: “How did you point to him? With your hand, with a nod of the head?”

### <span class="fac">[312\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/312.webp),[313\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/313.webp)</span>

“I alluded to him in my speech.” – “With which words?” – “I alluded to him when I spoke of a person who…” I alluded to him means approximately: I wanted someone to think of him when hearing these words. But “I wanted” is not the description of a state of mind, and “understanding that N was meant” is not either.

### <span class="fac">[313\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/313.webp)</span>

If I raise my arm ‘arbitrarily’, then I do not take _precautions_ for this purpose. Nor is the wish, the intention, such a precaution.

### <span class="fac">[313\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/313.webp)</span>

I do not wish that my arm might rise so that it may rise. (_Grammatical_ remark.)

### <span class="fac">[313\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/313.webp)</span>

If I move my arm arbitrarily, then I do not use (any) means to bring about the movement.

### <span class="fac">[313\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/313.webp),[314\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/314.webp)</span>

“Why did you raise your arm?” – “Because I wanted to.” – What kind of justification is that? Do I give a cause? A reason?

### <span class="fac">[314\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/314.webp)</span>

What does it consist of: to make the decision to give a certain sign?

### <span class="fac">[314\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/314.webp)</span>

Consider this description of an arbitrary action: “I make the decision to pull the bell at 5 o’clock; and when it is 5 o’clock, my arm simply makes this movement.” – Is this the right description, and not the one: “when it is 5 o’clock, I raise my arm”? – The first description one would like to add: “and behold, my arm rises when it is 5 o’clock.” And this “behold” is precisely what is missing here. We do _not_ say: “Truly, my arm rises!”

### <span class="fac">[314\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/314.webp)</span>

One could therefore say that the arbitrary movement is characterized by the absence of amazement. And now I do not want people to ask “But _why_ does one not marvel here?”

### <span class="fac">[315\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/315.webp)</span>

Language is an instrument. The concepts are instruments. Now one has the idea that it does not make a _great_ difference _which_ concepts I use. Just as one can do physics with feet and inches as with m and cm; the difference is only one of convenience. But that is not true either, if, for example, calculations in one system of measurement require more time and effort than we _can_ expend.

### <span class="fac">[315\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/315.webp)</span>

Concepts guide us to investigations. Are the expression of our interest and determine our interest.

### <span class="fac">[316\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/316.webp)</span>

05.05.1945

Imagine people who, when they _mean_ all sentences (assertions, questions, etc.), not only practice their pronunciation, or the like, _sing_ them. They say that the sung sentence “is alive”, the unsung one is dead. If these people philosophize about the concept of ‘meaning’, they will be tempted to say: meaning means singing.

### <span class="fac">[316\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/316.webp)</span>

Being unable to avoid saying this and that when engaging in philosophical thought, being irresistibly inclined to say this, does not mean being forced to make an _assumption_, or to immediately grasp something, or to know something.

### <span class="fac">[316\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/316.webp)</span>

“I leave the room because you command it.” “I do not leave the room because you command it.” Does this sentence describe a connection between my action and his command?

### <span class="fac">[317\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/317.webp)</span>

Under what circumstances does one say, “This device is a brake, but it isn’t working right now”? That means, after all, that it is not fulfilling its purpose. In what does its purpose consist? One could also say: “The _intention_ was that this should act as a brake.” Whose intention? Here, intention as a state of mind vanishes entirely. Could one not also imagine that five people have an intention and carry it out without any one of them having it? Thus, a government can have an intention that no individual has.

### <span class="fac">[317\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/317.webp),[318\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/318.webp)</span>

Anyone who perceives my expectation would have to perceive immediately _what_ is expected. That is to say: not _to_ _infer_ it from the perceived event! – But to say that someone perceives the expectation _makes no sense_. Unless, perhaps, he perceives the ‘expression of the expectation.’ To say of the expectant person that he perceives the expectation, instead of saying that he expects, would be a nonsensical distortion of the expression.

### <span class="fac">[318\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/318.webp)</span>

_Does it follow_ from the sense impressions I receive that there is an armchair there? – How can a _sentence_ follow from sense impressions? Well – does it follow from the sentences that describe the sense impressions? No. – But don’t I infer from the impressions that there is an armchair there? – I’m not drawing any conclusion! – But sometimes I am! For example, I see a photograph and say, “So there must have been an armchair there,” or even, “From what one sees there, I infer that an armchair stood there.” Well, that is a conclusion; but not one of logic. A conclusion is the transition to an assertion; and thus also to the behaviour that corresponds to that assertion. ‘I draw the consequences’ not only in words, but also in actions.

### <span class="fac">[318\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/318.webp),[319\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/319.webp)</span>

But was I justified in drawing these consequences?

What _is_ _meant_ by “justification” here? – How is the word “justification” used? Describe language-games! From them, the importance of justification can also be deduced.

### <span class="fac">[319\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/319.webp),[320\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/320.webp)</span>

One must not ask _what_ representations are, or what happens when one represents something, but rather: how the word “representation” is used. But that does not mean that I want to speak only of words. For insofar as my question concerns the word “representation,” it also concerns the question of the nature of representation. And I am merely saying that this question cannot be explained by pointing—neither for the person imagining nor for the other—nor by the description of any process. The first question also asks for an explanation of a word; but it leads one astray regarding the kind of answer that is to be expected.

### <span class="fac">[320\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/320.webp)</span>

Moore’s contradiction: “I believe it is raining, but it isn’t raining.” Let us imagine a language in which one says “It is raining” instead of “I believe it is raining,” but in a different tone of voice than the apodictic assertion “It is raining.” One could also create Moore’s paradox in this way: the statement “Perhaps it is raining” and the statement “It is not raining” do not contradict each other; but the statement “Perhaps it is raining, and it is not raining” is nonsense.

### <span class="fac">[320\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/320.webp),[321\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/321.webp)</span>

But one could also say: From the sentence “I believe it is raining, but it isn’t raining,” you should see, firstly, that it is raining; secondly (which does not contradict the first part) that I, L.W., do not believe it; – and if it is really raining, and I really do not believe that it is raining, then that sentence was true. – “But how could you have uttered it?” – A god spoke through me.

### <span class="fac">[321\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/321.webp)</span>

It is obvious: I can utter an assertion sentence (e.g., “It is raining”) without making an assertion. For example: if I read it in a story; if I utter it as a language exercise; if I repeat it to someone else, perhaps by considering what I will do; etc. It is an assertion if I utter it under certain circumstances (spatial and temporal environment).

### <span class="fac">[321\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/321.webp)</span>

One could use the Fregean assertion symbol _as follows_: When I write “He said: ‘It is raining’,” I do not yet know whether he wanted to assert this. Therefore, in this case, I will write “He said: ‘⊢ It is raining’,” which means “He asserted: ‘It is raining’.”

### <span class="fac">[321\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/321.webp),[322\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/322.webp)</span>

It is relevant here that, as I said earlier, one could express a contradiction as a sentence of logic; for example, in order to say what one should be wary of.

### <span class="fac">[322\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/322.webp)</span>

Think of an ironic utterance, e.g., the exclamation “He has no time!” – What one could add is: “what nonsense!” – And now one can also say: “He has no time! – He has _plenty_ of time!” & that is not a contradiction.

### <span class="fac">[322\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/322.webp)</span>

Imagine that people utter logical tautologies in a different tone than non-tautological sentences, and that they utter contradictions in an ironic tone.

### <span class="fac">[322\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/322.webp),[323\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/323.webp)</span>

“The will is free” (or “unfree”) is an assertion of a similar kind to the one that the future is predetermined (or that it is not). Not that the two say the same thing! But 1) they are both connected with our concept of causality; 2) they both express a _position_ of the human being towards events.

### <span class="fac">[323\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/323.webp)</span>

I often say: “I could not, or I did not want to (call it what you like!)” – “I could not” sounds like a justification or excuse; “I did not want to” sounds like obstinacy.

### <span class="fac">[323\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/323.webp)</span>

“I see myself drifting down a stream. If there is an overhanging branch that I could have held on to, then it was not my doing. If I did not grasp it, then it was not my doing either.” This expresses an attitude. And a different one, when I say: “If only I had kept my eyes open, I would have grasped the branch and saved myself!”

### <span class="fac">[323\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/323.webp)</span>

The philosopher treats a question; like an illness.

### <span class="fac">[323\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/323.webp),[324\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/324.webp)</span>

What does the remark “It is fate” mean? What does one communicate to someone with it? Nothing. And yet it is not empty talk, nor is it to be compared with a cry of pain. One might perhaps use a picture. Or an expression like: “It seems to me as if…” And then follows a simile, or the description of a picture. If one asks “What does this picture mean? Does it not only mean that you…?” – then the answer is: “It does not only mean this: i.e. I could just as well have used the interpretation instead of my simile. I _wanted_ to use a picture, and this picture.” – But why? – For no reason at all.

### <span class="fac">[324\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/324.webp)</span>

_Certainly_, all these things happen in you. – And now let me just understand the expression that we use. – The picture is there. And I do not deny its validity in the particular case. – Only let me now understand the application of the picture!

### <span class="fac">[324\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/324.webp),[325\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/325.webp)</span>

Imagine we always express the intention of a human being by saying: “He said, as it were, to himself: I want to…” That is the picture. And now I want to know: how is the expression “saying something as it were to oneself” used? Because it does not mean: saying something to oneself.

### <span class="fac">[325\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/325.webp)</span>

The picture is _there_; & I do not deny its _correctness_. But _what_ is its application? Think of the picture of the blind man:

![](../graphics/116,325.svg)

### <span class="fac">[325\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/325.webp)</span>

Man: a thinking, frail pipe. If one had called him a speaking pipe, it would have sounded absurd. But in this case, “thinking” should mean: conscious; having consciousness. That is, the emphasis here is on the paradox that a piece of matter can have consciousness. And yet there is no paradox here. Or: paradoxical is often human thought.

### <span class="fac">[325\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/325.webp),[326\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/326.webp),[327\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/327.webp),[328\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/328.webp)</span>

“The ending ‘a’ sounds different when it is the ending of a male name than when it is that of a female one. The ‘a’ in ‘agricola’ sounds different from that in ‘puella.’ The first sounds aggressive, the second soft.” I would also like to say that I can ‘_interpret_’ the final ‘a’ as feminine in one instance and masculine in another. And yet, of course, the ‘a’ _does_ not _sound_ different in the two cases. – But how can I read masculinity and femininity into the ‘a’? Well, the fact is that I am inclined to say these things, even though the masculine and feminine ‘a’ _do not_ sound different. When I say “The ‘a’ in ‘puella’ sounds feminine”—is there any way to verify that? Or: how else does it show itself, other than by my saying that this “a” sounds feminine? For example, one doesn’t say: “Listen closely when I pronounce it. You’ll hear that it sounds soft.” Nor is one taught to pronounce the “a” as feminine or masculine. It goes something like this: We learn that the “a” is the ending for the feminine gender; and that comes naturally to us, because countless female names end in “a” in our language as well. Then we learn masculine words ending in “a,” and at first that seems strange to us; but soon it becomes completely natural. We no longer think about the fact that although it sounds feminine, it is still a masculine noun. We find it quite natural to combine it with an adjective ending in “us.” We now _treat_ it as masculine, and it feels natural to us. When we think about the dual use of the ending for masculine and feminine, we also _associate_ with it gestures, mental images, and ways of pronunciation that we do not use in the ordinary use of words ending in “a.” Perhaps, however, in certain cases: for example, when we have _taken_ a word to be a woman’s name and it now turns out to be a male name. In this case, we often say: “Now the ‘a’ suddenly sounds different.” For by freeing oneself from the earlier conception, one does indeed make a special ‘masculine’ gesture and the like.

### <span class="fac">[328\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/328.webp)</span>

The interest of this case lies in the fact that it shows how we are sometimes inclined to interpret a difference in use as a difference in feeling.

### <span class="fac">[328\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/328.webp),[329\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/329.webp)</span>

The feeling of ‘familiarity’ & ‘naturalness’. – It is easier to find a feeling of unfamiliarity & unnaturalness. Or: _feelings_. For not everything that is unknown to us gives us an impression of unfamiliarity. And here one must consider what we call “unknown.” A fieldstone we see by the path, we recognize as such, but perhaps not as the one we have always seen there. A person, for example, as a person, but not as an acquaintance. There are feelings of familiarity; their expression is sometimes a glance, or the words “The old room!” (in which I lived many years ago & which I now see unchanged). Likewise, there are feelings of strangeness: I pause; see the object, or person, scrutinizingly & suspiciously; say “It is all strange to me.” – But because this feeling of strangeness exists, one cannot say that every object we know well & that does not seem strange to us gives us a feeling of familiarity. We feel, as it were, that the space once occupied by the feeling of strangeness must _somehow_ be filled. The space for this atmosphere exists, & if one does not occupy it, then another does.

### <span class="fac">[329\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/329.webp),[330\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/330.webp)</span>

Just as the German who speaks English well uses Germanisms, even though he never first forms a German expression and then translates it into English; just as he thus speaks English _as if he were translating_, ‘unconsciously,’ from German, so we often think _as if_ our thinking _were_ based on a process of reflection; as if we were translating from a very primitive mother tongue into our own.

### <span class="fac">[330\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/330.webp)</span>

When we _live_ within certain thoughts, they seem _important_ to us. And we forget that they may be of no interest whatsoever to others, just as the thoughts of earlier generations have become obsolete to us.

### <span class="fac">[330\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/330.webp),[331\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/331.webp)</span>

When we philosophize, we impose feelings where there are none. They serve to explain our thoughts. ‘_Here_ the explanation of our thinking demands a feeling!’ It is as if our conviction were acting in response to this demand.

### <span class="fac">[331\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/331.webp)</span>

Consider carefully how we use the word “recognize”! I recognize the furniture in my room, my friend whom I see daily. But no ‘re-recognition takes place’.

### <span class="fac">[331\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/331.webp),[332\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/332.webp)</span>

This is how the philosophical problem of the states & activities of the mind arises. First, the seemingly innocuous step is taken of speaking of something mental, whose more precise determination is provisionally left open. Precisely thereby, this step seems to be quite safe. However, the most important determination has already been made. And now one must revoke this step and seems thereby to say that these states of the mind do not exist at all. (So, no imagining, no thinking, no feeling, etc.)

### <span class="fac">[332\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/332.webp)</span>

The first step is the innocent, intuitive conception, whereby one (however) leaves the ‘nature’ of the processes or states open. The next step, however, is to see: whatever _the nature_ of this ‘something’ one wants to speak of may be—it gives no explanation and is a useless fiction. But if one now abandons this fiction, one seems to deny everything spiritual and thereby to say that there is only the physical.

### <span class="fac">[332\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/332.webp),[333\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/333.webp)</span>

“I” does not name a person, “here” does not name a place, and “this” is not a name. But they are connected with names. Names are explained by means of them. It is also true that physics does not use these words.

### <span class="fac">[333\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/333.webp)</span>

Seeing, hearing, thinking, feeling, and wanting are not, _in the same sense_, the objects of psychology as the movements of bodies, electrical phenomena, etc., are the objects of physics. You can see this from the fact that the physicist sees, hears, and thinks about these phenomena and communicates them to us, whereas the psychologist observes the _expressions_ (the behaviour) of his subject. (That one marries a woman and her money in a different sense, you can see from the fact that to marry her money means to marry her for the sake of her money.)

### <span class="fac">[333\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/333.webp),[334\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/334.webp)</span>

We speak of understanding a sentence in the sense in which it can be replaced by another that says the same thing; but also in the sense in which it cannot be replaced by any other. (Just as little as a musical theme.) In one case, the thought of the sentence is what is common to different sentences; in the other, something that only these words, in these positions, express. Understanding a poem.

### <span class="fac">[334\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/334.webp)</span>

So does “understanding” have two different meanings here? – _I_ would say that these ways of using “understanding” constitute its meaning, my _concept_ of understanding. For I _want_ to apply “understanding” to all of that.

### <span class="fac">[334\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/334.webp)</span>

But how can one provide an explanation of the ‘sense’ in the second case? Ask yourself: How _does_ one _lead_ someone to an understanding of a poem, or a topic? The answer to that question explains how one provides an explanation of the sense here.

### <span class="fac">[334\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/334.webp)</span>

A philosopher says: he understands the sentence “I am here,” he means something by it, he thinks something, – even if he does not remember how, on what occasions, this sentence is used. And when I say “The rose is red even in the dark,” you actually see this redness in the dark before you.

### <span class="fac">[335\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/335.webp)</span>

How does the philosophical problem of mental processes & states and of behaviourism arise? The first step is quite unobtrusive. We speak of processes & states and leave their nature open! We will perhaps know more about them at some point – this is assumed. But precisely thereby, one has committed oneself to a certain grammar. Because we have a certain concept of what it means to know a process more closely. (The decisive step in the card trick has been taken, and we have hardly noticed it.) And now the simile that was supposed to make our thoughts comprehensible collapses. We must therefore deny the still misunderstood process in the still unexplored medium. And so we seem to have denied the mental processes. And yet we naturally do not want to deny them!

### <span class="fac">[336\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/336.webp)</span>

Every sign seems dead _on its own_. _What_ gives it life? – It _lives_ in use. Does it have the breath of life within it? Or is _use_ its breath?

### <span class="fac">[336\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/336.webp)</span>

It is remarkable how much wish & fear play a role in human political thinking, & how little: knowledge.

### <span class="fac">[336\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/336.webp)</span>

Are we perhaps hasty in the assumption that a baby's smile is not pretending? – And on what experience is our assumption based?

### <span class="fac">[336\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/336.webp)</span>

We copy the numbers from 1 to 100, for example, & _conclude_, _think_, in this way. [Highly unclear.]

### <span class="fac">[336\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/336.webp),[337\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/337.webp)</span>

I could put it this way: when I copy the numbers from 1 to 100, how do I know that I will obtain a sequence of numbers that is correct when counting? And _what_ is a control here _for_? Or how should I describe this important empirical fact: should I say that experience teaches that I always count the same way? or that no number is lost when copying? or that the numbers remain on the paper as they are, even if I don't look? Or all these facts? Or should I say that we simply don't get into difficulties? or that everything usually seems to be in order? _That's_ how we think. _That's_ how we act. _That's_ how we talk about it.

### <span class="fac">[337\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/337.webp)</span>

In philosophy, no inferences are drawn. “It must be the case” is not a proposition of philosophy. It merely states what everyone concedes to it.

### <span class="fac">[337\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/337.webp),[338\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/338.webp)</span>

I can ‘look at the clock’ to see what time it is. But I can also look at a clock face in order to _guess_ what time it is; or for this purpose, adjust the hands of a clock that is not working until their position seems right to me. Thus, the image of the clock helps in (quite) different ways to determine the time.

So, drawing could help a person to remember an event correctly. Or the picture of a church could help to remember details of another church by helping us to see how it differed from our picture.

### <span class="fac">[338\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/338.webp)</span>

One could say: Whoever has given himself a private explanation of a word must now _intend_ internally: to use the word in such and such a way. And how does he do that? Should I assume that he invents the technique of this application, or that it already exists?

### <span class="fac">[338\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/338.webp),[339\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/339.webp)</span>

There is a psychology only for beings whose _behaviour_ is similar to that of humans.

### <span class="fac">[339\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/339.webp)</span>

The English noun “feel” (“it has a certain feel about it”) in contrast to “feeling”. For example, the word “feels like this and so”. For example, the use of the imperfect & the perfect in German; one sharper, more precise, the other broader, more comfortable. And yet it would be wrong to say: two different _feelings_ accompanied these (two) forms.

### <span class="fac">[339\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/339.webp)</span>

Think of someone who says: “I know how tall I am!” and at the same time points with his hand to his head! [“I am here.”] Or also: “Every person knows how tall he is.” [Connected with: “I” does not refer to a person; but also with the fact that something looks like a definition & is not (‘private ostensive explanation’) & with the example of the roller].

### <span class="fac">[339\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/339.webp)</span>

“To mean” means, so to speak: to be certain that one masters a use of the sentence. (One can always make detours into the surroundings of the sentence.)

### <span class="fac">[340\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/340.webp)</span>

One could speak of a vibrant life in the signs.

### <span class="fac">[340\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/340.webp)</span>

The play of the sign.

### <span class="fac">[340\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/340.webp)</span>

“While I was talking to him, I didn't know what was going on behind his forehead.” In this case, one does not think of brain processes, but of thought processes. It is a serious image. We would really like to see behind that forehead. And yet we only mean what we usually mean by it: “we would like to know what he is thinking”. I mean: we have the vivid picture there; & the use which, together with it, expresses the mental.

### <span class="fac">[340\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/340.webp)</span>

The _essence_ is expressed in the grammar.

### <span class="fac">[340\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/340.webp),[341\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/341.webp)</span>

The grammar says what kind of object something is. (Theology as grammar.)

### <span class="fac">[341\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/341.webp)</span>

“A property is a property” actually means that the word “property” is used as a word denoting a property. “Red is a property” means: “‘Red’ is a word denoting a property”; or, in a language without patterns:

R is a word denoting a property: where R → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mtext>Rot</mtext></menclose></math>. R → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mtext>Rot</mtext></menclose></math> is an ostensive definition.

### <span class="fac">[341\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/341.webp)</span>

If we now divide the properties into those that possess themselves, & those that do not possess themselves, then we divide the words denoting properties into two classes. If “f” is a word denoting a property & we give it the name “φ”, then we explain:

φ → “f” φ → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>f</mi></menclose></math>

### <span class="fac">[341\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/341.webp),[342\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/342.webp)</span>

I now want to say that f does not possess itself. – Thus, while “property” denotes one of the properties, “f” does not have the designation of one of the fs. Or

(x) : f(x) ⊃ { x ≠ φ, where <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mtext>φ</mtext></menclose></math> → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>f</mi></menclose></math> }

(x) : f(x) ⊃ x ≠ φ, where <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mtext>φ</mtext></menclose></math> → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>f</mi></menclose></math>

### <span class="fac">[342\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/342.webp)</span>

And the property _ε_&#x27;, which is supposed to be the property of a property not to possess itself, can thus be defined as follows:

ε&#x27;(f) = (∃a):.(x):f(x) ⊃ x ≠ a where <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mtext>a</mtext></menclose></math> → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>f</mi></menclose></math>

And ε&#x27;(ε&#x27;) = (∃a) :. (φ) : ε&#x27;(φ) ⊃ φ ≠ a where <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>a</mi></menclose></math> → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mtext>ε'</mtext></menclose></math> =

= (∃a):∙:(φ)∷(∃b) :. (y): φy ⊃ y ≠ b where b → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mtext>φ</mtext></menclose></math>

f(f) = (∃x): [ f(x) where <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>f</mi></menclose></math> → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>x</mi></menclose></math> ]

~ ∙ f(f) = (x): (f(x) <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>f</mi></menclose></math> → <math display="inline"><menclose notation="box"><mi>x</mi></menclose></math>

### <span class="fac">[342\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/342.webp),[343\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/343.webp),[344\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/344.webp)</span>

Imagine someone said: “Joy is a sparkle of the soul.” – That sounds much more correct than—as James does, for example—to say that it is a sum of sensations, the perceptions of our body’s joyful behaviour. The former is an image, an illustration, which does not yet prejudge the use of the term ‘joy.’ The second leads us down the wrong path. But why? The concept of ‘joy’ is not similar to that of a sensation. – When I hear that someone is joyful, I am not interested in any feelings in their chest, around the corners of their mouth, in their eyes, or in their muscles. Yes, even if I say I have a joyful feeling in my chest, the other person is not actually interested in the feeling in my chest. – But don’t they interest _me_? – In any case, they are not what I wish to communicate. – “I am happy” is above all an _expression_ of joy. It can be compared to clapping, cheering, laughing, etc., but it is not a description of this behaviour; nor is it a description of the sensations that might cause this behaviour. [“One cannot guess how a word is used.”]

### <span class="fac">[344\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/344.webp)</span>

“Everything that happens _must_ happen.” – For it cannot happen _any_ _more_ than it does. And therefore it cannot be any less than _it must happen_.

### <span class="fac">[344\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/344.webp)</span>

We are interested in the that, not the why. But we do not lose anything from the world.

### <span class="fac">[344\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/344.webp)</span>

“So can I not point to my image?” – Of course, I can point to it. But what am I doing then?

### <span class="fac">[344\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/344.webp)</span>

Compare: Playing a chess game in one’s head – playing a soccer match in one’s head. Doing a calculation in one’s head – doing a chemical experiment in one’s head.

### <span class="fac">[344\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/344.webp)</span>

Now imagine a board game (“board soccer”). Each player has the figures that represent the players of one of the two teams. There is also a ball that is used.

### <span class="fac">[345\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/345.webp)</span>

When someone communicates that they believe in God, that is a very vague communication. “But ‘believe’ simply means ‘to hold to be true’!” – Yes, it means to hold to be true, & this definition does not provide any explanation that interests us. I believe that the weather will stay nice, that the Earth revolves around the Sun, that my behaviour back then was bad.

### <span class="fac">[345\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/345.webp)</span>

“So is the communication ‘I believe…’ in itself uninteresting?” – Not if we know the context.

### <span class="fac">[345\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/345.webp)</span>

The first & third person of psychological verbs.

### <span class="fac">[345\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/345.webp),[346\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/346.webp)</span>

The Fregean assertion sign indicates the beginning of the sentence. It thus has a similar function to the period. It distinguishes the entire period from the sentence within the period.

If I hear someone say “it is raining,” but I don’t know whether I have heard the beginning & the end of the period, then this sentence is not yet a means of communication for me.

### <span class="fac">[346\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/346.webp)</span>

The first & third person of psychological verbs. In certain trains of thought, it seems to us as if they then have two different meanings. But one can also say: What makes their peculiarity is that these two aspects are fused together into one unit.

### <span class="fac">[346\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/346.webp),[347\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/347.webp)</span>

How do I know that _this_ train of thought led me to this action? Well, it is a certain picture: e.g., in an experimental investigation, being led to an experiment through a calculation. It looks _like_ this: … & now I could describe an example.

### <span class="fac">[347\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ms-116/347.webp)</span>

_Trinity College_

_Cambridge_

_02.08.1945_

Dear Kari,

I have to write to you in English because my Norwegian is so bad that I am afraid you might not be able to understand it. Thanks ever so much for writing to me. I longed to hear from you & all my friends in Skjolden. I wish you had written a little more about how everybody is. You didn't mention Arne Draegni & Arne Bolstad, or Froeken Rebni. Please …
